| 1.38 | 1 Sep 2026 | Closes v1.37's defect. **The cause is paging, confirmed on a device**: the FR-803 event query at `maxResults=1` returned `items=0` **with a `nextPageToken`**, while the identical query at 250 returned `items=2`. A filtered `events.list` applies `privateExtendedProperty` to *a page of the scan* rather than using it to select the page, so asking for one event asks Google to test one arbitrary event against the filter — and to say so honestly by offering a token the client never followed. FR-803's note claimed "one request answers the question however large the calendar is"; that was wrong in exactly the way that matters, and it is why AC-07 passed on 27 Aug, when the calendar held about one event. **The fix is the loop, not the number.** `findEventBySourceHash` now follows `nextPageToken` to exhaustion and reports `scanCapped` when it runs out of pages — the rule the task scan already carried, that a search which stopped looking must never be read as one that found nothing. Raising 1 to 250 would have fixed this calendar and left correctness a function of how much the user keeps in theirs. Verified on the device through the production path: `found=true`, `scanCapped=false`, while the same calendar still answers `items=0, nextPageToken=YES` at `maxResults=1`. **Recorded about method, twice over.** The old assertion `maxResults=1` passed the whole time the query it described was writing duplicates — a test can pin a URL exactly and still pin the wrong one. And the paging loop lived inside a REST client owning its own HTTP, so nothing could serve it a page; it is now extracted to take its `get`, which is the same reachability lesson as the drain's own untested check, one layer down. No requirement changed; FR-803's how-it-is-met note is corrected by this entry. |

| 1.39 | 1 Sep 2026 | Records a defect found while setting up FR-806's confirmation run: **an offline save can block indefinitely in token resolution instead of failing fast into the queue.** Observed on a device — Save tapped in aeroplane mode, Play services reporting `getToken() -> NETWORK_ERROR` four seconds later, and then **thirteen minutes of nothing**: no error, no queue entry, no change on screen. The mechanism is precise and is a hole between two designs that are each correct alone. `accessToken()` falls through to `authorize()` when the cached token has expired; offline, Play services cannot refresh silently and returns a result carrying `hasResolution()`; the client then suspends in `AuthResolutionBridge.resolve()`, waiting for an Activity to launch the consent `PendingIntent`. **Only `MainActivity` attaches that bridge** — deliberately, so a consent screen survives the rotation that destroys the Activity which launched it — and a capture is not `MainActivity`. So the resolution waits for something that will never come. Attaching the bridge afterwards, by opening the app, releases it: the intent launched, failed immediately with `ApiException 15` offline, was classified `GoogleUnreachable`, and the capture queued correctly. So the capture is **stranded rather than lost**, and any later launch of the app recovers it — which makes this a capture that silently stops until the user happens to open Latch, rather than an NFR-302 loss. **AC-10's fail-fast is therefore unverified for an expired token**: its pass on 28 Aug was within a session whose token was still cached, so `authorize()` was never called. The third path this week found to have never actually run. **The fix shape, recorded before it is built**: a capture must never attempt interactive resolution. Where a resolution is required and no Activity is attached, `authorize()` shall throw a retryable failure so FR-806 queues the capture exactly as a network error already does — the consent being a thing to ask on next launch, not a thing to wait on behind a sheet the user has already dismissed. No requirement changed. |

| 1.40 | 1 Sep 2026 | **Adds FR-806a**, turning v1.39's recorded defect into a requirement before it is fixed. A capture shall never block on an interactive authorization: where a consent resolution is required and no Activity is attached to present it, the attempt fails as **retryable** so FR-806 queues the capture, as a network error already does. **The drain side is decided in the same breath, because the two rules differ and would otherwise be conflated.** A drain runs online by construction, so a silent refresh is expected and a resolution should not arise; if one does, the entry is **held rather than permanently failed** — it is not a failure waiting cannot fix but one a *sign-in* fixes, and giving up would lose a capture the user could have recovered in a tap. The queue then says **"Sign in needed"** beside the pending count, on the surface FR-806 already requires it to be visible on, so opening the app resolves the consent and the next drain proceeds. This is the one case where an entry waits on the user rather than the network and must look like neither a failure nor an ordinary wait. **AC-10's fail-fast half is recorded as never having been verified for an expired token** — its 28 Aug pass was inside a session whose token was still cached, so `authorize()` was never reached — and a debug-only token-invalidation hook is added so that half can be run in a minute: invalidate, aeroplane mode, capture, expect "queued" within a second and the home screen counting it, then reconnect and watch the entry retire. No requirement changed; FR-806a is added. |

| 1.41 | 1 Sep 2026 | Settles the two halves of the defect v1.34 grouped as one seen twice — a screenshot's furniture is not its content — before either is built. **Chrome-bleed gains its rule**: blocks lying **wholly** within the top **4%** of an image are dropped in `:ocr`, over bounding boxes, before the text is assembled. A fraction rather than a pixel count, because a status bar is three to five per cent of any screen this app will meet; wholly-within rather than overlapping, because discarding a real first line is the risk that deferred this fix in the first place. The consequence is stated rather than left to be discovered: the rule is **inert below roughly 1000 pixels of image height**, since a block cannot sit wholly inside 4% until 4% exceeds a line of text — so a cropped screenshot whose first line begins at the very top passes through untouched, and that is a guarantee held by a fixture. **Bottom chrome is recorded as observed and deliberately unhandled**: the composer placeholder and navigation bar are the same class, but the arithmetic does not transfer, because the bottom of a screenshot is where the most recent message sits and a symmetric rule would risk dropping the very content the user captured. **FR-509a is added**: an OCR-derived capture takes its title from the row carrying that item's own date, falling back to the preceding row and then to FR-509 unchanged, so a title is never empty. That is a requirement rather than a refinement because FR-805b had already excluded the sender's name and the amount paid from the description on the same capture, and FR-509 put both back into the **title** — the field a list shows, a notification quotes and every device syncs. **And the `item_key` question v1.23 opened is closed explicitly**: the key's input stays separate from the display title, §7.2's derivation is unchanged, one capture still yields one key and a chain still shares it. Per-item keys were considered and rejected — they would break the one-key property and turn FR-804's tie-break from what a message is about into which occurrence, for a purely cosmetic gain — so this is **not** a wire-contract move and items written before it stay matchable. No requirement changed; FR-509a is added. |

| 1.42 | 1 Sep 2026 | Closes the first of the two improvements NFR-101a recorded as owed to the document case, and confirms three records rather than adding them. **Page-level progress is built**: while a PDF is being read the confirmation screen says "Reading page N of M…" instead of "Reading the text…", the information having existed at the point the loop runs and simply not been surfaced. **M is the number of pages that will be read, not the number in the document**, and that is a reading rather than an oversight: this line is a progress indicator over work, so counting to fourteen while stopping at ten would show a bar that never fills, and FR-207's cap is separately and explicitly reported after the fact by the "first 10 of 14 pages read" line — which is where the requirement puts it. The steps are a pure function of the page count so the sequence is testable without a PDF, the arrangement `pagesToRead` already has. **NFR-102 is what this serves**, not NFR-101a's budget: the budget is unchanged and still owed a re-measurement on mid-range hardware. **Skipping the second recogniser** for a document whose first page shows no Devanagari remains owed and deliberately unmeasured. Three earlier records are confirmed present rather than restated: the one-capture-one-key decision (v1.41 and FR-509a), the cropped-screenshot guarantee with its stated ~1000-pixel minimum height (v1.41, held by a fixture in `OcrDecisionsTest`), and bottom chrome as deliberately unhandled (v1.41 and `withoutTopChrome`'s KDoc). No requirement changed. |

| 1.43 | 1 Sep 2026 | Lands FR-701's storage and the FR-700 Capture Inbox, and settles the readings each needs before either is built. **Storage is a hand-rolled `SQLiteOpenHelper` with per-record encryption under the existing `KeystoreCipher`**, on the migration discipline the write queue already carries — a leading version, an unreadable record dropped rather than mis-parsed, and older layouts still read where dropping one would lose something. Room stays deferred on the build-configuration grounds `docs/DEPENDENCIES.md` records; nothing here needs a query language that `WHERE hash = ?` does not cover. **The write queue does not move into it.** SRS 1.24's per-item written marker is carried on the queue record itself, at record version 4, rather than by migrating the queue: a queue entry holds a capture that exists nowhere else, and migrating one to tidy the storage layer risks precisely what NFR-302 forbids, for no gain the user could see. **FR-803's local index of source hashes is stored in the clear**, and that is a decision rather than an oversight: a SHA-256 digest is not content — NFR-206 says exactly that of `latch.source_hash` in a Google item — and an index that must be decrypted row by row to be searched is not an index. Everything beside it that *is* content stays encrypted. **FR-512's interim reading is retired**, as v1.10 said it would be the moment this landed: a capture below the threshold, one with no date at all (FR-506 row 4, AC-03), and one that cannot be completed (FR-506 row 3) are routed to the Inbox instead of written, and the confirmation screen's button says **Add to Inbox** rather than Save so that routing is a thing the user chose and not a save that quietly went somewhere else. **A notification-derived capture is never routed to the Inbox**, and this is the third appearance of one rule rather than a new one: the Inbox is persistent storage, NFR-206 forbids notification content reaching it, and FR-805a and FR-210a already exclude the same layer from the item description and from webhook delivery. The consequence is recorded as a **narrowing and not a deferral** — FR-512's interim behaviour, confidence shown rather than the save blocked, survives permanently for that layer alone, because the alternative is to lose the capture and design principle 1 forbids that above everything. **The Inbox re-parses at the captured instant, never at the instant it is opened.** It stores the raw text and the capture's own `now` and zone, which is what keeps `:data` free of `:parser`; re-parsing against today's clock would move "kal" one day further every time the user looked at the list, which is design principle 1's failure inverted — not inventing a date, but silently changing one. **FR-804's offline limitation gets the cure its own note named.** Where there is no network and the local index shows the same `latch.item_key` at a different resolved date, the capture is **held in the Inbox** rather than queued as a create: FR-804 forbids a silent update and a drain has no user to ask, so the question waits for a surface, and the Inbox is that surface. When it is finally asked, the prior state is read from **Google at match time** and never from the index. **The index is deliberately not used to cure FR-804's capped task scan.** A row records what Latch wrote, not what the item holds now, and SRS 1.19 requires an update's prior state to be what the item held before this app changed it — restoring an index row would overwrite a hand edit with a value the user was never shown. That residual stands. **FR-807's offer now survives process death**, which its own note deferred to this storage, and moves to the home screen once the capture window has gone; two of its three recorded limits stand unchanged — a new capture still ends the offer, and undo from another device is still a different feature. **FR-806 gains three things it was recorded as lacking**: a **backoff ceiling** of thirty minutes, so a queue that failed overnight is not waiting five hours in the morning; an **immediate drain** when the OS reports connectivity restored or any foreground request to Google succeeds, applied **only to entries whose last failure was transport-class** and rate-limited to once a minute, because a 429 or a 500 is not cured by knowing the socket works; and a **Retry now** button beside the pending count, which is also the manual retry FR-806's note says a given-up entry has none of until Settings exists. **AC-03 becomes reachable** for the first time. No requirement changed; FR-701 to FR-705 are met, FR-512's interim reading ends, and FR-506 row 3's own picker is still owed. |

| 1.44 | 2 Sep 2026 | Closes the three confirmation-screen requirements FR-807's note has listed as unmet since v1.12 — FR-506 row 3, FR-507 and FR-510 — and records the readings each needed. **FR-506 row 3's picker is presented inline and already unfolded, not as a modal that opens over the sheet.** The requirement says the picker "opens automatically with suggestion chips"; the chips are therefore on screen without a tap, and the full calendar is one tap behind them. A modal calendar appearing unbidden over a floating capture window would cover the very text the user is being asked to confirm, which is the opposite of what a confirmation screen is for, and the requirement's word is *opens*, not *covers*. The chips are **Today, Tomorrow and In a week**, and they are not an inference: design principle 1 forbids the *app* choosing a date, and a chip the user taps is the user choosing one. **FR-507's single control is the badge itself.** FR-508 already requires the EVENT/TASK badge to be visible at all times and, in a chain, on every item; making that badge the control puts the override where the classification is shown and satisfies "a single control" literally. It is per row for the same reason the badge is. **Overriding an Event to a Task discards its time, and the screen says so before the tap rather than after.** §8.1 is the reason — the Tasks API records only a date — and this is the one place in the app where a user action deliberately loses something they wrote, so it is disclosed rather than performed quietly. **A date range overridden to a Task loses its end date**, which is the same §8.1 limitation that made v1.23 classify a range as an Event in the first place; it is allowed because FR-507 says the user *shall* be able to override, and disclosed for the same reason. **FR-507 also supplies a second way out of FR-506 row 3**: a time with no day, made a Task, is an undated task and needs no date at all — recorded because it is a consequence rather than a design, and a reader meeting it later would take it for an accident. **FR-510 is applied per candidate, not per capture.** Its wording — "where the **only** date found is in the past" — was written when a capture produced one item; with FR-511 a capture can hold a past date beside a future one, and writing the past one as a dated item merely because it was not the only date would produce exactly the outcome this requirement exists to prevent. **The follow-up is an undated task.** A follow-up needs a date only if the app picks one, and picking one is what design principle 1 forbids; "tomorrow" would be the guess AC-04 exists to catch. **There is no opt-out, and that is the requirement rather than a narrowing**: "shall **not** create a dated item" admits no dated item at all, so the offer is take-it-or-leave-it and leaving it is unticking the row — which FR-511's checkboxes already provide. **FR-510 outranks FR-507** for the same reason: a past row cannot be overridden into an Event, because that is precisely the dated item the requirement forbids. The past date is recorded in the item's notes above the FR-805 body, carried on `Item.notes` and added to §7.1's queue record at version 5. **AC-04 becomes reachable** for the first time. No requirement changed; FR-506 row 3, FR-507 and FR-510 are met, and FR-807's "three other requirements remain unmet" note is now stale rather than wrong. |

| 1.45 | 2 Sep 2026 | Wires `:recipes` to a screen — FR-601 to FR-608 — and resolves a contradiction this specification has carried since v1.16. **§5.8 and §7.2 disagreed about a recipe chain's `latch.item_key`, and §7.2 governs.** FR-804's note says "an `item_key` is per-item and a recipe's steps carry different titles and therefore different keys"; §7.2 at v1.23 says step 2 blanks *every* date span so that "every item a single capture produces derives the same key", and adds that the alternative was considered and rejected. The two cannot both be true of a recipe chain. **v1.23 is later, is explicit, and is what the client implements**, so the §5.8 sentence describes a derivation that was withdrawn and is corrected here rather than left to be discovered by whoever builds the second client. The consequence is unchanged in substance: an `item_key` query still returns one item per chain, so FR-804 still updates the matched item and not its chain — which is now true because a chain shares one key rather than because its steps have different ones. **A recipe is offered only for a capture holding exactly one date**, which is the same narrowing v1.25 took for FR-804 and taken for the same reason: FR-601 expands *one* captured date, and a capture with four raises a question this specification has never asked — which of them anchors the chain, or whether four chains are produced, and what FR-807's undo then groups. Offering the chooser and silently anchoring on the primary would be FR-205's multi-image failure again: accept the gesture, act on one of the things, say nothing about the rest. The reason is shown on screen rather than expressed as a missing control. **Applying a recipe routes to Google whatever FR-512's threshold says.** Picking a template against a date visible on screen is a stronger confirmation than the threshold is testing for, and routing it to the Inbox would discard the chain the user had just built. **FR-607 holds structurally**: `recipeItems` is the single place a chain's destination is chosen, so "all items in a chain shall be written to the same calendar" is not something a call site can forget. `Recipe.targetCalendarId` is deliberately **not** honoured yet — routing a save to a calendar the user has not seen on the confirmation screen is what FR-906 forbids outright, and the picker that would fix it arrives with FR-905's routing rules in Settings. **FR-603 never mutates a built-in.** FR-602's eight ship as code, so editing one stores a copy carrying its id which *shadows* the shipped version; deleting that copy restores the shipped one, and deleting a recipe of the user's own removes it. That keeps the shipped set upgradable for everyone who has not customised it, without inventing a second concept for a customised one, and makes "reset to the shipped version" a delete rather than a feature. A duplicate always mints a new id, including from a built-in — a copy that kept the id would shadow rather than sit beside. **§7.1's `Item` gains reminder minutes and §7.2's `latch.recipe` is written for the first time.** A recipe step's reminders had nowhere to go: `RecipeStep.reminderOffsets` existed and no write path read it, so FR-602's "meeting with preparation" shipped a thirty-minute reminder that never reached a calendar. Reminders are written as explicit overrides and **omitted entirely where there are none**, which leaves Google's own calendar defaults in force — an empty override list would mean "no reminders at all", a decision this app has never taken for the user, and the two requests are indistinguishable in the field alone. Tasks get none: Google Tasks has no reminder of its own, which is §8.1's limitation one field over. **FR-1001's settings record is added ahead of its UI**, holding the working week, the holiday additions and removals FR-605 requires, the date order, the default duration and reminder lead times, the FR-512 threshold, the time zone, FR-1003's per-layer toggles and FR-905's routing rules — with defaults that are, field for field, the behaviour the app already had, so an unreadable record is a working app with some preferences forgotten rather than a broken one. It decodes **field by field** where a value is unrecognised and all-or-nothing only on the record version, which is the opposite trade the account-defaults store makes and for the stated reason: losing one preference is better than losing all of them, while a layout this code does not know cannot be read one field at a time either. **AC-06 becomes reachable.** No requirement changed; §5.8's item_key sentence is corrected, FR-601 to FR-608 are met, and FR-905's rules are stored but not yet applied. |

| 1.46 | 2 Sep 2026 | Builds the FR-1000 series, including FR-1004, and records what shipping the webhook now obliges. **FR-1004 ships, so FR-1004a, FR-1004b, FR-210a, FR-1102's privacy-policy clause and FR-1103's Data Safety declaration all bind.** The first three are built and tested; the last two are **documentation obligations that fall due at submission** and are added to FR-1108's release gate, because they are things only the publisher can do and the distance between this decision and that submission is exactly the distance over which an obligation is forgotten. **The capability is off by default and the endpoint is a second, separate act** — FR-1004 asks for both ("disabled by default" *and* "requires the user to enter the endpoint URL explicitly"), and keeping them as two controls means a paste cannot start sending anything. **FR-1004a's list is enumerated in code rather than serialised from a type**, and that is the decision rather than a style: a payload assembled by serialising `Item` would grow silently the next time `Item` did, and what would leak is whatever was added. **Plaintext endpoints are refused outright rather than warned about**: the address receives the user's captured content, and over `http` that is not a decision a warning makes reasonable. **The webhook does not go through `ALLOWED_HOSTS`, and must not.** That guard holds AC-17 for every request this app composes; widening it to carry a user endpoint would destroy the property it exists to hold. A webhook is sent by a separate client with its own narrower rules — HTTPS only, no credentials in the authority, no redirects, one attempt, short timeouts — and the separation is what keeps AC-17 and AC-18 testable as different criteria. It also means FR-1004b's "shall not be placed in the FR-806 write queue" holds **structurally**: everything in that queue drains through the guard and would be refused. **FR-905's rules are applied, and FR-904's second half is built.** The destination is now decided by one pure function that the confirmation screen and the saver both call, which is what makes FR-906 — "never route silently to a calendar the user has not seen" — a property rather than a promise; a screen that worked it out separately would eventually show one calendar and write to another. The calendar list is fetched **on demand and not on open**, because NFR-101 budgets the whole capture path 800 ms and a `calendarList.list` round trip would spend most of it on a control almost no capture uses. Lowest priority wins and ties go to the user's own order, so the same capture cannot route differently on different launches. **Task lists are deliberately not routed**: §8.2 records that they cannot be coloured or hidden individually, so routing between them would buy the user nothing they could see, and FR-607 wants a chain in one place. **FR-907 is built and both its halves are kept**: three overrides from one source produce an *offer*, and nothing is written by counting. The count is per source application, because that is what a rule matches on. **FR-1003's toggles are honoured differently by layer, and the screen says so.** The Quick Settings tile is its own manifest component, so switching it off disables the component and the tile leaves the shade. Text selection and the share sheet are three intent filters on **one** activity, so disabling it would remove all three at once; those toggles are enforced when the capture arrives, which means Latch still appears in the share sheet and declines with a reason. Splitting the activity per layer is the cure and is recorded as owed rather than pretended away. **FR-1001's settings reach the parser**, which is where FR-504's date order, the default event duration and FR-512's threshold have always belonged. One consequence is recorded rather than left to be found: **on a cold start the first frames of a capture use the shipped defaults**, because the settings record is read asynchronously and a capture may be the first thing in the process; the sheet re-parses when it arrives, which is NFR-102's own pattern, and Save is blocked meanwhile by the destination read, which is a round trip to the same store. The alternatives were a blocking read on the main thread or an asynchronous text path, and NFR-102's note is explicit that the risk of adding one is that it quietly captures the synchronous one. **AC-12 is met by what Settings does not do**: switching between Option A and Option B writes one field and touches nothing else — no item is read, moved or deleted, and the other mode's stored choice is not cleared. FR-1002 also asks for that to be *stated*, and it is. No requirement changed; FR-1001 to FR-1004b and FR-905 to FR-907 are met, FR-1005's `.ics` export is not in this slice, and FR-1102's and FR-1103's webhook clauses become live obligations. |

| 1.47 | 2 Sep 2026 | Builds the three requirements left standing on their own — FR-908, NFR-205 and FR-1005 — and records what each needed decided. **FR-908's failure case is a failure to read the list, not a missing calendar, and that governs.** `calendarList.list` fails for want of a network far more often than because a calendar was deleted, so an unreadable list changes **nothing**: falling back to the primary calendar because the phone was on a train would move a user's captures for a reason having nothing to do with their calendars, and would then need a second launch, online, to discover it had been wrong. A stored destination is presumed good until the list says otherwise. **"Missing" and "lost write access" are one test rather than two**, because FR-901 already filters the list to `owner` and `writer` — a calendar the user can no longer write to is simply absent from it. **A renamed or recoloured destination is corrected silently**: it has not moved, and telling someone their own rename went through is noise; FR-904's chip is meant to look like Google. Only the fallback informs the user, which is FR-908's own word and the one case where their captures are about to start landing somewhere they did not choose. The check runs off the launch path entirely and gates nothing. **NFR-205 is one action with an order that matters.** The revoke goes **first**, because it needs a token: deleting local data first would leave nothing to authenticate with and the grant standing in the user's Google account for ever — the half they cannot fix afterwards without knowing to go to `myaccount.google.com`. The local deletion then happens **whether or not the revoke succeeded**, because the user asked for their data gone and a network failure must not leave it; the outcome says which half worked, which is NFR-303's instinct applied to something that is not a write. **Play services has no revoke to call** — `AuthorizationClient` grants authorization and offers nothing to take it back, and `GoogleSignIn.revokeAccess` belonged to the sign-in API this app deliberately does not use — so the revoke is the OAuth2 endpoint, through the same `ALLOWED_HOSTS` guard as every other request. `oauth2.googleapis.com` has been on that list since the guard was written, against exactly this call, so the allowlist does not move and AC-17 is untouched. **A 400 from the revoke endpoint is treated as success**, on the same reasoning `alreadyGone` applies to a delete under FR-807: the caller asked for a grant not to exist, and Google answers 400 for a token it does not recognise. **The local deletion is enumerated rather than swept.** A directory wipe would be shorter and would silently start missing stores the day someone adds one — which for this requirement means a user asking for their data to be deleted and some of it staying — so adding a store is a change to that function, visible in a diff. **FR-1005 is treated as a wire format**, because it is one: the reader is somebody else's calendar program and there is no second chance to correct a file a user has already emailed. CRLF endings, RFC 5545 §3.1 folding at 75 **octets** — not characters, or a Devanagari title would be cut through the middle of a UTF-8 sequence and produce a file nothing can decode — and §3.3.11 escaping with the backslash escaped first. **A task exports as a `VTODO`, not as an all-day `VEVENT`**: the two are different things and most readers show them differently, so exporting a to-do as a day-long appointment would put a block on someone's calendar that Latch never created. **An undated task exports with no `DUE` at all**, which is design principle 1 reaching as far as a file format. **An exported item carries no §7.2 metadata**, and that is a decision: those keys are a contract between §4.1's three clients writing to one Google account, they mean nothing elsewhere, and an `X-` property carrying `latch.source_hash` would put a digest of the user's own message into a file they may send to somebody else. **An export mints fresh item ids**, because a `UID` is what a reader uses to decide whether an import is a new item or a replacement — the ids a save uses are stable by design, and stable is exactly wrong here: two exports of one capture would have the second import silently overwrite the first. **The exported file goes out through a `FileProvider` rooted at one cache subdirectory**, not at internal storage, and the directory is cleared on every export so at most one file is ever waiting. It is deliberately **not** enumerated by NFR-205's deletion: it is a copy made to hand to another app, in the one directory the system clears on the user's behalf. No requirement changed; FR-908, NFR-205 and FR-1005 are met, and `AuthClient.signOut` stops being a no-op. |

| 1.48 | 2 Sep 2026 | Builds the notification listener, FR-208 to FR-212, and records four readings and one permission the requirements do not name. **`POST_NOTIFICATIONS` is added, and it is the app's first runtime permission.** FR-211 requires the layer to post a low-priority notification offering to capture; since Android 13 posting one needs that permission, so without it the offer would be created and silently dropped — a feature that appears to do nothing. It is worth separating from the access this layer is about: it does not let Latch **read** anything. It is requested from the FR-209 disclosure screen at the moment the user turns the layer on, never at launch, because a permission prompt on first run for a feature that is off by default is the pattern that teaches people to refuse prompts. Found by lint before a device could find it. **The message text is held in the process's memory and the posted notification carries only a key.** This is how FR-210 and NFR-206 are met **structurally**. The obvious implementation — the message in the `PendingIntent`'s extras — fails both: a posted notification's extras are held by the system's notification manager, which is not this app's memory and not this app's to reason about, and no file is written anywhere along the way. A process death therefore loses an outstanding offer, which is correct rather than a limitation: memory that survived a process would be storage by another name. The holder is bounded at twenty, because a listener sees everything and an ignored offer must not keep its text alive for the life of the process. **FR-212's list of applications is built from what the listener has seen.** Offering a picker of installed applications needs `QUERY_ALL_PACKAGES`, a restricted permission this specification will not spend on a settings screen, so the picker offers the packages that have posted a notification while access was granted. **A package name is not notification content**, which is what NFR-206 governs — the requirement is about the message, and a package name is how the system addresses an app. Nothing is ticked by default: FR-209 has the layer off, and a layer switched on that immediately began reading every messaging app would be a second decision the user never made. **Every filter is a pure function, and the listener holds none of them.** Nothing inside a `NotificationListenerService` is reachable from a JVM test, which is the exact shape that hid the drain's FR-803 check and `decodeBitmap`'s null contract for a slice each — and what these filters decide is which of the device's notifications Latch reads at all, which is the part of this feature a mistake in would be worst. The listener's own notification is skipped **first**, before the layer toggle and before the monitored list are even consulted: FR-211 has this layer post a notification, and a listener that read its own would offer to capture its own offer for ever. **The disclosure screen carries two statements the SRS asks for by name.** FR-210a: "The suppression shall be stated in the notification-access disclosure screen." FR-805a: "The exclusion shall be stated in the notification-access disclosure screen alongside FR-210a's." Both are properties of `CaptureSource` in code and no implementation can forget either; what could be forgotten is telling the user, which is what those two lines are. The screen also states the honest limit — Android grants notification access per app, not per notification, so Latch is technically able to see notifications from applications the user has not chosen and simply does not act on them. **AC-19 and AC-22 are now reachable, and both hold as properties rather than as code in this layer**: `webhookEligible`, `storesSourceText` and `routableToInbox` all exclude `NOTIFICATION`, so a notification capture sends no webhook, stores no source text and never reaches the Inbox, whatever this file does. FR-1104's Play Console justification is added to FR-1108's release gate, being the half only the publisher can do. No requirement changed; FR-208 to FR-212 are met, and `POST_NOTIFICATIONS` joins the manifest. |

| 1.49 | 2 Sep 2026 | Discharges the half of FR-1108 that lives in the repository, and writes down the half that does not. **The signing configuration reads a git-ignored file, and its absence is not an error.** `keystore.properties` names the keystore and holds its passwords; a committed `.example` beside it shows the four keys with no values. A config with the passwords inline would put the upload key's credentials in a history they cannot be removed from. The absence being tolerated is the deliberate part: NFR-503 requires the build to be reproducible from a clean checkout, and every debug build and the launch canary have to work on a machine that has never seen a keystore — so a release build with no properties file produces an **unsigned** artifact rather than a failed one, and shipping one is caught by the release checklist rather than by the compiler. **No keystore is generated by anything in this repository**, deliberately: a key a build script made is a key whose passwords something other than the publisher has seen. **The `bundle` block enables ABI, density and language splits**, which is the condition NFR-103's entire per-device reading rests on — 13.97 MB against 42.58 MB, on a 40 MB budget, and the smaller figure is only true if Play actually delivers a split. **`docs/RELEASE.md` is added** and holds the six gates in one place: the two that are done, and the four that only the publisher can do — creating the keystore, taking a **bundletool-derived** per-device measurement to replace the estimate NFR-103 currently rests on, registering the release SHA-1 against the Android OAuth client, and the three Play Console declarations FR-1102, FR-1103 and FR-1104 now require. It records one fact that catches people out and is nowhere else in this specification: **where Play App Signing is enrolled, Play re-signs the artifact**, so the certificate reaching users' devices is not the one uploaded with — and it is that second fingerprint the OAuth client needs, not only the upload key's. Without it, sign-in fails on exactly the build being shipped, which is FR-1108 item 3's own warning met one level of detail further down. No requirement changed; FR-1108's items 1 and 2's *block* are met, and items 2's measurement, 3, and the FR-1102/FR-1103/FR-1104 declarations are recorded as owed to the publisher. |

| 1.50 | 2 Sep 2026 | Adds an instrumented suite beyond the launch canary, and records what it is for and what it is deliberately not. **It exists for one class of defect, and the class is now well evidenced: a platform call whose stubbed behaviour under JVM unit tests inverts the real one.** `BitmapFactory` is a throwing stub in the `android.jar` unit tests compile against — the same property that makes the parser corpus cheap and lets FR-105's reducer be tested in milliseconds — and it hid a dead image path through a whole slice with 328 tests green throughout. `SQLiteOpenHelper`, `SharedPreferences` and the Android Keystore have exactly that standing, which is why the FR-701 slice shipped every record format tested and none of the storage under them. **The first three tests are the three defects the FR-215 device pass found**, each asserting the signature its own failure had: `decodeBitmap` returning `UNREADABLE_SOURCE` for a readable PNG, a Bengali codepoint inside Latin text, and an FR-805b extract that is not strictly shorter than what was recognised — with the second half of that last one, which a length check alone would miss, asserting that the sender's name and the amount paid are gone. **The remainder covers `:data`'s storage, which had never run at all**: the Inbox row and its captured instant and zone, the FR-803 index, FR-807's stored offer with the prior dates an undo must write back, the recipes, the settings, NFR-203's secret and its mask, one queue entry per chain, a drain retiring rather than writing, and SRS 1.24's per-item marker surviving to disk. **Espresso and Compose UI test remain absent, and adding either is still a new NFR-501 decision** — every assertion here is over a store, a file or a recogniser, which is the class a device can settle and a JVM cannot; an assertion about a screen belongs in a pure function the screen calls, which is where every decision in this app already lives. **The suite leaves the app installed.** `connectedAndroidTest` uninstalls both APKs when it finishes, which for this project has teeth: the device is then left with no Latch and the next thing anyone does is a device pass that needs one, so a run meant to raise confidence takes the app away. It is an AGP-injected Gradle property because there is no DSL for it; if a future AGP drops it the symptom is the old behaviour rather than a build failure, so the check is that the app is still there afterwards. **Run on a device the same day, and the prediction in this row's first draft came true.** All five OCR tests failed with `FileNotFoundException` — the suite read its fixtures from `targetContext`, which is the *app under test's* assets, while `src/androidTest/assets` is packaged into the **test** APK and reached through `getInstrumentation().context`. A suite that has never executed is a suite whose fixtures may not be reachable; they were not. Fixed, and **21 of 21 pass in 13.3 seconds** on a Pixel 6 Pro. The result worth recording is the fifteen storage tests: `:data`'s SQLite, every store's round trip through `KeystoreCipher`, one queue entry per chain, SRS 1.24's per-item marker surviving to disk, and a drain retiring without writing — none of which had ever run. Two limits on what that says. **Nothing in the suite touches Google**: every API in it is a fake and the OCR half reads two bundled assets, so no acceptance criterion in §10 is closed by it. And the storage tests write and read **within one process**, so they prove the SQL and the cipher and not survival across a restart — NFR-302's app-termination limb is still owed. No requirement changed. |

| 1.51 | 2 Sep 2026 | **Adds FR-509b and amends FR-507, both from the first real image capture anyone made.** A photograph of a schedule table produced a correct date, a correct time and a correct classification under the title **`Location Notes`** — two column headings. FR-509a takes an OCR title from the row carrying the item's own date and falls back to the preceding row; in a chat that row is the message, and **in a table it is the header**. The rule behaved exactly as specified and gave the wrong answer, which is the third time this specification has met the same shape: the status-bar clock, the sender's name, and now a column heading. Every rule here that says "the text" was written when text meant what a person typed. **The answer is not a better derivation.** Chat, table, receipt and letterhead are an open set, and each new heuristic buys one layout and risks another — the chrome-bleed rule was deferred an entire slice because dropping a band can also drop a real first line. **FR-509b closes the class instead of the instance**: the title is editable on the confirmation screen, before anything is written, so no automatic derivation has to be load-bearing. **It must not move `latch.item_key`**, and that is the clause with teeth: §7.2 derives the key from the captured text with date spans blanked and writes metadata once at insert, so a corrected title that moved the key would make the item permanently unmatchable by FR-804 — a later reschedule would create a second item — and two users correcting one garbled capture differently would derive different identities for the same message. FR-206's subject line stays the key's input, because it comes from the sending application rather than from a correction, which is §7.2's own step 1 and not an exception invented here. **A defect of exactly that kind is fixed in the same change**: the Capture Inbox's FR-702 title edit was routed through `preferredTitle`, which §7.2 returns verbatim, so editing a title there **was** silently moving the key. **FR-507 gains a second sentence — the control shall be discoverable.** Its override is the EVENT/TASK badge, which satisfies "a single control" literally; what it did not do was say so, and the user asked for the ability to change an item's type while looking at a screen that offered it. A string explaining the tap had been written and rendered nowhere. A requirement met by a control nobody can find is not met. **One layout defect is recorded alongside them**, found in the same capture and fixed before it: the confirmation sheet had grown from a title, a date and two buttons into something capable of a dozen sections, with no scroll and no separation, and on a device at a large font scale **the Save button was below the glass and unreachable**. The content now scrolls and the action row does not — a scroll alone would have left Save reachable *after* nine recipe chips, which is not the same as reachable. The recipe chooser folds away behind a single control for the same reason: a recipe is the exception, and nine lines on every capture to serve the few that want one is the wrong trade. No requirement is removed; FR-509b is added and FR-507 is strengthened. |

| 1.52 | 2 Sep 2026 | **Starts the Windows client of §4.1, and the first decision is which language it is written in.** Three requirements name Windows machinery by name — FR-302 registers a hotkey through `RegisterHotKey`, FR-303 extracts text through `Windows.Media.Ocr`, FR-305 ships an MSIX — and the obvious reading of those three is a C# application. **It is written in Kotlin on the JVM instead, and the reason is §7.2.** That clause says the `item_key` derivation "is the one part of §7.2 that depends on parser behaviour rather than on arithmetic over the text, and it is therefore where three independently written clients are most likely to drift", and its own remedy is a file of conformance vectors. Vectors detect drift; they do not prevent it, and the drift they are detecting is silent by construction — a second client that blanked a date span one character differently would derive a different key from the same screenshot, and the visible symptom is a phone moving an item while a desktop creates a second one, with no error anywhere. **Sharing the compiled derivation removes the failure instead of catching it.** That option exists only because `:parser`, `:recipes` and `:core-model` were kept free of Android from the first commit for a different reason — FR-501 and NFR-502 — so a desktop JVM consumes them unchanged. A benefit taken here was paid for months ago. **The three Windows-native primitives were probed before the decision, not after**, because a decision resting on "presumably reachable" is how a slice discovers in its last hour that it cannot ship: `Windows.Media.Ocr` recognised the Android OCR fixture correctly in **109 ms** against ML Kit's 878 ms on the phone; `RegisterHotKey` is reachable through the C# compiler that ships inside Windows, needing no SDK installed; and DPAPI round-trips a secret for NFR-203. **No new dependency, and the UI is Swing rather than Compose Desktop** — Compose Desktop would be a dependency under NFR-501 carrying tens of megabytes of Skia natives into NFR-103's 80 MB, and against that FR-304 wants full keyboard operability and NFR-401 wants a screen reader, which is the one comparison Swing wins outright through the Java Access Bridge. **`:wire` is added: the write contract as a module rather than as prose.** §7.2's metadata and hashes, FR-509/509a/509b's title derivation, FR-805's description and FR-1005's `.ics` move into it out of `:data` and `:app`, and both clients compile the same file. What deliberately does **not** move is everything a client is free to do its own way — HTTP, JSON, storage, UI — because sharing those buys nothing and would drag Android's platform into a desktop build. The §7.2 key names stop being `internal`: they are the contract, and a second client reads and writes those exact strings. **The conformance vectors still run**, and their job is now the different one they were always better at — pinning the derivation against *change over time*, which shared code does not address. **One asymmetry between the clients is recorded rather than discovered later**: Android bundles ML Kit's Devanagari model and pays 12.83 MB for it, while `Windows.Media.Ocr` reads only the language packs the machine has installed — the development machine offers `en-GB`, `en-US`, `ko` and two Chinese variants and **no Devanagari at all**. NFR-404's Hinglish is romanised and unaffected, but a Devanagari image that captures on the phone will not capture on the desktop, and FR-303 must say so on screen rather than return an empty capture. No requirement changed; `:wire` is added and FR-300's implementation is begun. |

| 1.53 | 2 Sep 2026 | **Moves the Google client into a module both clients of §4.1 compile, and writes the JSON by hand to make that possible.** The Windows client needs to talk to Google, and the obvious plan was to write it a client of its own. FR-803's duplicate query is the argument against: it was written once, was wrong in a way no test could see — a filtered `events.list` returns an empty page carrying a `nextPageToken`, so asking for one result almost never finds the match — and it wrote duplicates into a real calendar for five days before the cause was found, over two days of diagnosis. **A second client with its own copy of that query is a second chance at exactly that defect**, and AC-07 requires the two to find each other's items, so a disagreement between them is invisible by construction rather than merely likely. One implementation cannot disagree with itself. **The only thing standing in the way was `org.json`**, which ships inside `android.jar` and nowhere else, so the choice was a dependency or a few hundred lines — and the surface actually used is twenty methods. It is hand-written, as the REST and the RFC 5545 export already are, and for the same reason. **Its names are `org.json`'s, method for method, deliberately**: it made moving an 826-line write path a device has already verified a change of two import lines rather than a rewrite of every call site, and a diff a person can read is worth more than a nicer API in the code that writes to a user's calendar. **Two JSON semantics are load-bearing and are tested as such.** `put(name, null)` **removes** the key, which is how §7.2's `latch.recipe` is absent rather than null for a capture with no recipe — the 27 Aug 2026 device pass checked for exactly that absence. And `JSONObject.NULL` writes an explicit null, which is a *different instruction* rather than a spelling of the same one: `tasks.patch` clears a due date only when the field is present and null, so FR-807's undo of an update depends on being able to say the difference. **Reading is deliberately not symmetrical** — a null in a response is indistinguishable from an absent key, because nothing needs the distinction and offering it would invite code that came to depend on it. **The throwing accessors are kept alongside the `opt` ones and named apart**, because a client reading somebody else's API should degrade on a field that changed shape while a test asserting about a body should fail rather than compare two empty strings; this specification already records what a test that pins the wrong behaviour costs. **`FailureClass` moves with the client** — classifying a failure is the client's job, not the queue's — and **AC-17's `requireGoogleEndpoint` and `ALLOWED_HOSTS` become public**, which is the requirement's own instinct rather than a concession to the compiler: NFR-201 is "every outbound request goes through one check", and a check callers outside one file cannot reach is a check that grows a second, unguarded path the first time somebody needs one. | 1.54 | 2 Sep 2026 | **Builds FR-002's grant for Windows, and records the asymmetry it creates with Android.** There is no Play services on a desktop, so this client performs the authorization-code exchange itself: RFC 8252's loopback redirect, PKCE with S256, and a `state` value checked before anything else in the callback is even read. **The consequence is that the Windows client stores a secret where the Android client stores none**, which is a real difference rather than an oversight. `CLAUDE.md` records that no access token is persisted on Android *because* Play services holds the grant and re-authorizing a granted scope set returns a token with no UI — NFR-203 is satisfied there by there being nothing at rest. The receiver binds to the **loopback address explicitly** on an ephemeral port and serves exactly one request — binding to all interfaces would put an authorization endpoint on the network for the length of a sign-in. | 1.133 | 6 Sep 2026 | **Asked whether photographing a card from a different distance changes what is read, and whether cropping or deskewing is owed. Yes to the first, no to the second, and the arithmetic found something in our own code.** **Framing dominates, and it dominates more than it should because of a decode this project chose for memory.** `inSampleSize` accepts only powers of two, so a 12.2 MP photograph (4032 x 3024) cannot be decoded at 3/4 or 2/3 — it is decoded at **half on each axis, 2016 x 1512**, to stay under `MAX_DECODED_PIXELS`. The card's text is then read from whatever share of 2016 pixels the card occupies. For a 89 mm card with roughly 1.8 mm capitals: **41 px of glyph if the card fills the frame, 20 px at half the width, 13 px at a third, 10 px at a quarter.** ML Kit's Latin recogniser is reliable to somewhere around 16–20 px and degrades sharply below it — so a card photographed at arm's length is not *slightly* worse, it is on the wrong side of a cliff. That is the mechanism behind SRS 1.123's two readings differing, and it was seen directly on 5 September: the FR-1203 fixture photographed far enough away to include a monitor's surroundings did not decode its QR **and** pulled a terminal window into the classifier's notes, while the same card filling the frame decoded cleanly. **Cropping and deskewing are not owed, for three reasons in order of weight.** ML Kit v2 already detects text at arbitrary in-plane rotation, so a card photographed crooked is handled; what it does *not* do is document edge detection or perspective correction, and neither does anything else in this project. The remedy for both is free — fill the frame, hold the phone parallel — where a document scanner is a **new dependency** under NFR-501 and an interactive full-screen UI besides. And a crop cannot recover resolution that the decode has already discarded, so it would be treating the symptom. **What the arithmetic did find is worth acting on and is not acted on here.** The halving buys memory the app then does not spend: 2016 x 1512 as `ARGB_8888` is **12 MB against a 32 MB budget** — barely a third — because the power-of-two step from 1 to 2 has no intermediate. Decoding at **`RGB_565`** would put the full 4032 x 3024 in **24 MB**, inside the same budget, and hand the recogniser **four times the pixels**: 82 px of glyph at full frame and 27 px at a third, which moves the marginal case to the right side of that cliff. Text recognition does not need alpha and ML Kit works in greyscale internally, so the loss is colour depth this path never uses. **It is recorded and not changed**, because it touches every capture including screenshots and this project's standing rule is that an OCR change is measured on a device rather than reasoned about — the Devanagari artifact of 31 August is what that rule was bought with. **An instrument ships instead**: `MlKitOcrReader` now logs `decode WxH sample=N -> WxH` under the usual debug guard, so the next photograph states its own numbers rather than being estimated from a specification sheet. Dimensions are not content. |

| 1.136 | 6 Sep 2026 | **Auto-cropping is the right answer and v1.133 gave the wrong one, because it did not distinguish cropping *before* the decode from cropping after it.** The developer's argument is that phones differ in aspect ratio and that filling the frame by hand every time is not a thing software may require, so the app should crop. That is correct and the arithmetic is decisive **once the ordering is right**. A crop applied to the decoded bitmap recovers nothing — the pixels are already gone, which is what v1.133 was reasoning about. A crop applied *to the source*, by re-decoding only the card's region at `sample=1`, recovers all of them: a card at **half** the frame reads at **41 px of glyph, identical to one filling it**; at a third, 27 px instead of 14; at a quarter, 21 px instead of 10 — across the threshold in both cases. It costs **2–10 MB** against the 32 MB budget the whole-frame decode spends. `BitmapRegionDecoder` has been in the platform since API 10 and reads JPEG, so this needs no dependency. **Crop to the recognised text, not to the card's edges**, and that is the substance of this row. Edge detection means either ML Kit's Document Scanner — a new dependency under NFR-501, a Play-services module wanting a network on first use, and an interactive full-screen UI that would replace FR-1201a's flow entirely — or hand-rolled contour finding, which is a great deal of fragile code against a patterned desk. **Neither is needed, because the app does not want the card's edges; it wants its text, and it already has the boxes.** ML Kit returns a bounding box per block in pass one, and their union is the region worth re-reading. It is indifferent to the background, to the card's colour, to whether the card is even rectangular, and to the phone's aspect ratio — which is the developer's own point, met without asking anybody to detect a document. **The skew fix composes with it**: block corner points give the dominant text angle, so the second pass can rotate the region and hand `inReadingOrder` an upright image, which is the root cause of SRS 1.134's scrambling rather than the symptom v1.134 routed around. **The cost is one extra recognition and it is bounded**: pass two runs only where the text union is small enough for it to matter, so a card already filling the frame pays nothing, and the region is smaller than the frame so the pass is cheaper than the first. | 1.137 | 6 Sep 2026 | **FR-1228 is built: a photographed card is read a second time at the resolution of its own text.** Pass one is unchanged. The **guard** compares widths in the text's own axis and demands a **half again** before spending a second recognition, so a card already filling the frame pays nothing. The **winner is the longer text**, which is a deliberate choice of failure: the region comes from pass one, so a pass that found only a corner would send pass two to re-read that corner, and preferring the longer reading makes that cost nothing instead of losing what was already read. And **every failure returns null and keeps pass one** — an unreadable format, an allocation refused, a degenerate rectangle — because FR-1228 is an improvement and never a precondition. **`sourceRect` is where three coordinate frames meet and it is tested hardest**, because getting it wrong throws nothing: it would re-read a different part of the photograph at high resolution and return confident nonsense that looks like bad OCR. The property test — every rotation mapping a full frame back to the whole file — is what would catch a reflection or a swap, and it caught the author's own arithmetic being wrong in a hand-written expectation while the code was right. **Deskew is not in this slice and is still owed**: `TextBlock` carries a box and not corner points, so the dominant text angle is not available yet, and SRS 1.134's row-grouping collapse is still routed around by the email rule rather than fixed. **Built, JVM-tested and installed; the second pass has not been seen firing on a device.** |
| 1.138 | 6 Sep 2026 | **FR-1228's second half: the writing is levelled before it is read again, which is SRS 1.134 fixed at its cause rather than routed around.** `TextBlock` gains an `angle`, taken from ML Kit's **corner points** — those describe the text's own quadrilateral where `boundingBox` is the axis-aligned rectangle around it, and on turned writing only the corners know. `dominantTextAngle` is the **median**, because one block read crookedly (a logo caught as a character, a mark on the desk) would drag a mean and cannot move a median; boxes narrower than 40 px are dropped first, a two-character box having almost no baseline to measure. Below **two degrees** nothing is done, since `inReadingOrder` tolerates a slight tilt and the rotation would cost an allocation for nothing. **The two rotations compose in one `Matrix` and the order is the correctness**: the region was decoded from the *file*, so it still needs the EXIF turn to be upright, and the angle was measured in that upright frame, so it is subtracted afterwards. Where the bitmap is physically turned ML Kit is told `rotationDegrees = 0`; where it is not, the EXIF rotation still has to reach it — getting that pair wrong would rotate twice or not at all, and neither throws. **What this does and does not buy, stated because the developer asked.** It makes reading *order* independent of how the card was laid down, which is what decides the name, the title and every positional rule. It does **not** make recognition itself angle-proof: pass one still has to find the text well enough to locate it and measure it, and ML Kit's own accuracy falls away at large arbitrary angles. So the four square orientations and a generous tilt are handled; a card at 45 degrees rests on pass one being good enough to bootstrap the correction, which is an empirical question and is **owed a device measurement**. |

So every photographed card claimed to hold one unsaved date, and the branch that closes the capture could never be reached from a card. **Settled by running the parser over the recognised text rather than by reading the code** — `candidates=1, classification=TASK_UNDATED, date=null` — which is this project's own instrument discipline applied to a UI question. `unsavedDates` counts `date != null` and is the pure function the decision now reads. **`date != null` rather than the classification**, deliberately: FR-510's past-date follow-up is an undated *item* derived from a date the user actually wrote, it keeps that date on the candidate, and filtering on `TASK_UNDATED` would have dropped it — the same defect pointing the other way. The device row that would have caught it has been open in the backlog since 4 September and names this exact failure, *being dropped onto an empty capture sheet after finishing*; it was never run, and the two fixtures it asks for are why. **The new test is the card itself**, parsed by the real parser, and was confirmed to go **red** against the old expression rather than assumed to bite. |

FR-807's ten-second offer stays first for the reason it was already first — a control that pushed the countdown down the screen would spend it. **The dates section has no button and that is its point.** This pillar has no home-screen entry at all: FR-203 to FR-207 are reached from other applications, and the note is the whole of what this screen can offer it — until it had a heading of its own that note read as a caption for the card button above it, which is SRS 1.113's misplaced-caution defect on a second screen. **A rule above each heading rather than a card around each group**: four borders would compete with the one filled button the screen has, and FR-1201a's card action is the thing that must stay findable. **No requirement moved and none was added.** Every FR comment is carried across unchanged; this is presentation, and it is recorded because the reasoning behind a layout is exactly what a later reader cannot recover from the layout. |

**How to read this document.** Requirements are numbered (FR-nnn functional, NFR-nnn non-functional) so they can be quoted, tracked and tested individually. Requirements marked **[MUST]** are in scope for v1.0. Those marked **[SHOULD]** are expected but may be deferred by agreement. Those marked **[LATER]** are explicitly out of scope for v1.0 and are recorded here only to prevent architectural decisions that would block them. A requirement marked **[MUST, if X ships]** is conditional: it does not compel X to be built, but binds absolutely if X is built.

---

# 2. Introduction

## 2.1 Purpose

This document specifies the requirements for a free, cross-platform utility that captures dates, deadlines and to-dos from text or images encountered anywhere on a phone or PC, and writes them to the user's Google Calendar and Google Tasks.

## 2.2 Problem statement

Commitments arrive as unstructured text scattered across messaging apps, email, web pages, PDFs and images. Transcribing them into a calendar is manual, so they are frequently missed. Existing tools solve fragments of this: screenshot-to-calendar apps handle images but send them to vendor clouds; task managers offer quick capture but no image handling and no system-wide text selection; browser extensions cover web pages only. No single product covers phone and PC with a unified capture model and on-device processing.

## 2.3 Product vision

One capture gesture on every surface the user touches; one calendar at the other end; and nothing leaves the device except the finished calendar entry — unless the user has deliberately configured it to (FR-1004).

## 2.4 Definitions

| Term | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **Capture** | A single user action that sends selected text or an image to the app |
| **Item** | One thing the app will create — either an Event or a Task |
| **Event** | A Google Calendar event. Has a start time, and may have end time, location, attendees, reminders |
| **Task** | A Google Tasks entry. Has a title, optional due **date** (no time — see §9.1), optional notes |
| **Recipe** | A user-selectable template that expands one captured date into a set of related items |
| **Chain** | The set of items produced by one save. Usually a Recipe expansion (FR-601), but a multi-date capture (FR-511) and a save of a single item are also chains, of several and of one — FR-807 undoes "all items created by that save", so every save needs a group identity, not only those a Recipe produced |
| **Capture Inbox** | Local holding area for captures that are incomplete, undated, or low-confidence |
| **Latch calendar** | A dedicated Google Calendar created by the app at setup (the default destination) |
| **Source** | The application a capture originated from (mail client, messaging app, browser, etc.) |

## 2.5 Reference material

Two animated walkthrough videos (Android and Windows) accompany this document and illustrate the intended first-run setup and capture flows. Where the video and this document differ, **this document governs**.

---

# 3. Product overview

## 3.1 Target users

Primary: general consumers in India managing school, family, travel, payment and appointment dates arriving through messaging apps and email. Secondary: professional users capturing meeting and deadline commitments from email and documents.

## 3.2 Design principles

These are binding and should be treated as acceptance criteria in their own right.

1. **Never invent a date.** If no date is found, the item is created undated. The app must never silently default to "today" or any other guess.
2. **On-device only.** Text and image content is never transmitted to any server operated by the publisher, nor to any third party other than Google, and then only as the finished calendar, task or contact entry. The sole exception is an endpoint the user has explicitly configured themselves under FR-1004, which is disabled by default.
3. **No backend.** The publisher operates no server that receives, stores or processes user content.
4. **Show the destination.** The target calendar is always visible before saving and always changeable in one tap.
5. **Reversible.** Anything the app creates can be found and undone as a group.

## 3.3 Non-goals

The product is not a calendar client, not a task manager, and not a note-taking app. It does not display a calendar view beyond what is needed to confirm a save. It does not attempt to replace Google Calendar or Google Tasks.

---

# 4. System architecture

## 4.1 Overview

```
  Android app  --+
  Windows app  --+-->  Google Calendar API
  Browser ext  --+     Google Tasks API
                            |
                            v
                   User's Google account
```

There is no publisher-operated backend. Cross-device consistency is achieved because all clients write to the same Google account; **Google Calendar is the synchronisation layer**.

## 4.2 Client responsibilities

Each client independently performs: capture, on-device text extraction (OCR where applicable), parsing, classification, recipe expansion, and the API write. No client depends on another being present.

## 4.3 Google Cloud configuration

**FR-001 [MUST]** A single Google Cloud project shall host three OAuth client credentials: Android (package name + SHA-1 release fingerprint), Desktop, and Chrome Extension.

**FR-002 [MUST]** The application shall request only the following scopes:

| Scope | Purpose | Classification |
|---|---|---|
| `.../auth/calendar.events` | Create and update events | Sensitive |
| `.../auth/calendar.calendarlist` | Read the user's calendar list | Sensitive |
| `.../auth/calendar.calendars` | Create the Latch calendar | Sensitive |
| `.../auth/tasks` | Create tasks and read task lists | Sensitive |
| `.../auth/contacts` | Create and update contacts (§5.11, FR-1200 series) | Sensitive |

**FR-003 [MUST]** No scope granting access to Gmail, Drive (except as noted in FR-1006) or any other Google service shall be requested. **Contacts was removed from this prohibition at v1.84** when §5.11 was approved; the reversal is recorded here rather than only in the revision history, because a prohibition that quietly stops applying is one nobody can audit. The narrowest sufficient scope remains the rule, and §5.11.2 records that the People API offers no create-only alternative. The developer shall confirm the minimum sufficient scope set against current Google documentation before submitting for OAuth verification, as scope granularity changes over time.

---

# 5. Functional requirements

## 5.1 First-run setup (FR-100 series)

**FR-101 [MUST]** On first launch the app shall present a three-step setup: (1) Google sign-in, (2) calendar destination, (3) task list selection.

**FR-102 [MUST]** Step 1 shall state plainly, on screen, that the app does not read the user's mail or messages and stores nothing on publisher servers.

**FR-103 [MUST]** Step 2 shall present exactly two options:

- **Option B — "Create a Latch calendar" (default, pre-selected, marked Recommended).** A new Google Calendar is created and becomes the destination for all captures.
- **Option A — "Use my existing calendars".** Captures are routed to the user's existing calendars per §5.10.

**FR-104 [MUST]** Step 2 shall explain the benefit of Option B in the user's terms: one colour, one show/hide checkbox in Google Calendar, and the ability to undo a batch.

**FR-105 [MUST]** The Latch calendar shall be created via `calendars.insert` **only on completion of setup**, not on entry to step 2. If the user abandons setup, nothing shall be created in their Google account.

**FR-106 [MUST]** Step 3 shall list the user's task lists via `tasklists.list` and allow selection of a default.

**FR-107 [MUST]** Step 3 shall disclose that Google displays all task lists within a single Tasks layer in Google Calendar, and that task lists cannot be colour-filtered in the way calendars can.

**FR-108 [MUST]** Setup shall be completable in under 60 seconds with no text entry beyond Google authentication.

**FR-109 [MUST]** Every choice made during setup shall be changeable afterwards in Settings, and the setup screens shall say so.

**FR-110 [SHOULD]** The app shall support more than one Google account, with an account switcher available at capture time. Calendar and task list defaults are stored per account.

## 5.2 Android capture (FR-200 series)

Four independent capture layers are required. Each must function if the others are disabled.

### Layer 1 — Text selection

**FR-201 [MUST]** The app shall register an activity with an `ACTION_PROCESS_TEXT` intent filter (`category.DEFAULT`, `mimeType text/plain`) so that it appears in the system text-selection toolbar.

**FR-202 [MUST]** The activity's `android:label` shall be short enough to display without truncation in the floating toolbar (target: 6 characters or fewer).

**FR-203 [MUST]** The activity shall read `EXTRA_PROCESS_TEXT` and honour `EXTRA_PROCESS_TEXT_READONLY`.

**FR-204 [MUST]** Documentation shall note that host applications targeting Android 11 or later must declare a `<queries>` element for `PROCESS_TEXT` for the action to appear, and that this is outside the app's control. Known non-cooperating apps shall be listed in an in-app help topic.

### Layer 2 — Share sheet

**FR-205 [MUST]** The app shall register as a share target for `text/plain` and `image/*`.

> **`ACTION_SEND_MULTIPLE` is deliberately not registered, and this is a decision awaiting an answer rather than an omission.** This requirement names `text/plain` and `image/*` in the singular, and the singular is what is built. Several images shared at once is not a larger version of one image: it is a **multi-capture** question this specification has no answer for. Each image would be its own recognition, its own parse, its own set of FR-511 candidates, its own `latch.chain_id` and its own FR-807 undo — and §2.4 defines a chain as the items produced by *one save*, which four unrelated screenshots are not. Nothing in the FR-500 or FR-800 series describes a confirmation screen holding several independent captures, or what one undo over them should mean.
>
> **Registering the filter without settling that would be worse than not registering it.** The app would appear in the share sheet for a multi-image selection, accept it, and silently read whichever image happened to be first — the user having watched their other three go nowhere, with nothing on screen to say so. That is the same failure mode as accepting a file the app cannot read, which is why the `image/*` and `application/pdf` filters themselves waited for FR-215 rather than being declared ahead of it.
>
> **What would settle it**, when it is taken up: whether several images are one capture with several sources or several captures confirmed together, and what FR-807's undo groups in each case. The Capture Inbox (FR-700 series) is the natural home for the second reading, since it already exists to hold captures a user has not finished with.

**FR-206 [MUST]** Where the sending app supplies `EXTRA_SUBJECT` (typical of mail clients), the subject line shall be preferred as the item title over the shared body text.

**FR-207 [MUST]** The share target shall accept `application/pdf` and extract text from it. (See FR-215.)

> **How this requirement is met, and what it gives up.**
>
> **The platform renders; FR-215's recogniser reads.** `android.graphics.pdf.PdfRenderer` has
> been in Android since API 21, well below the minimum SDK FR-516 fixes at 26, so a PDF page
> becomes a bitmap at no dependency cost and goes through the same recogniser as a shared
> image. One path serves both kinds of PDF, which is the point: a scanned PDF has no text
> layer to read and would need this path regardless.
>
> **What that gives up is real and is recorded rather than glossed.** A PDF that *has* an
> embedded text layer carries its text exactly, and a library that read that layer directly —
> PdfBox-Android, or iText — would extract it perfectly where OCR of a picture of the same
> page will not. **The fidelity loss is accepted.** Such a library is 5–16 MB on a budget
> FR-215 has already spent a third of, iText brings an AGPL-or-commercial licence question,
> and neither removes the need for the OCR path. The consequence to expect is ordinary OCR
> error — a mis-read digit in a date, a column order the recogniser reconstructs differently
> from the page — on documents where an exact answer was available and was not taken.
>
> **Ten pages, and the cap is reported rather than applied silently.** Rendering and
> recognising is per-page work with a per-page cost, and NFR-101 budgets 2.5 s for a single
> full-screen image; an unbounded PDF is an unbounded wait. The app shall OCR the first **ten**
> pages and shall **state on the confirmation screen** how many of how many it read — "first
> 10 of 34 pages read" — so that a date on page eleven is a thing the user is told about
> rather than a thing that silently was not there. This is the instinct FR-803's
> `scanCapped` already establishes: a search that stopped looking must say so, because the
> caller cannot otherwise tell it apart from having looked everywhere and found nothing. The
> number is a reading and is movable by revision; what is not movable is that the cap is
> visible.

### Layer 3 — Notification listener

**FR-208 [MUST]** The app shall optionally implement `NotificationListenerService` to detect dates in incoming messages.

**FR-209 [MUST]** This layer shall be **disabled by default**, enabled only through an explicit in-app disclosure screen that names what is accessed and why.

**FR-210 [MUST]** Notification content shall be processed entirely in memory, never written to disk, and never transmitted. Only a user-confirmed item is persisted.

**FR-210a [MUST]** Webhook delivery under FR-1004 shall be **suppressed for any capture originating from this layer**, whether or not a webhook is configured. Rationale: this layer carries the heaviest disclosure obligation under FR-1104, and forwarding notification-derived content to an arbitrary third-party endpoint is inconsistent with the basis on which the user grants notification access. The suppression shall be stated in the notification-access disclosure screen.

**FR-211 [MUST]** When a date is detected, the app shall post a low-priority notification offering to capture it. It shall never create an item without confirmation.

**FR-212 [MUST]** The user shall be able to select which applications this layer monitors.

### Layer 4 — Quick Settings tile

**FR-213 [MUST]** The app shall provide a `TileService` that, when tapped, brings a capture activity to the foreground and reads the clipboard. This is the supported path for apps (notably WhatsApp) where partial text selection is unavailable and the user must use "Copy".

**FR-214 [MUST]** The app shall not attempt background clipboard access, which is restricted from Android 10 onward.

### Image and document capture

**FR-215 [MUST]** Images and PDFs received by any layer shall be processed with on-device OCR (ML Kit Text Recognition v2 or equivalent), supporting at minimum Latin and Devanagari scripts.

> **How this requirement is met, and what meeting it commits the project to.**
>
> **ML Kit Text Recognition v2, bundled, with the Latin and Devanagari models in the APK.**
> ML Kit ships in two forms with an identical API: *bundled*, where the native pipeline and
> the models are packaged into the application, and *unbundled*, where a thin client
> downloads both from Play services on first use. The choice between them is the whole
> decision, and it was settled by measurement against NFR-103 — recorded in full, with the
> method and the per-ABI breakdown, in `docs/DEPENDENCIES.md`.
>
> | Variant | Universal APK | Per device (arm64-v8a) |
> |---|---|---|
> | Unbundled, Latin + Devanagari | +325 KB | +325 KB |
> | **Bundled, Latin + Devanagari** | **+41.41 MB** | **+12.83 MB** |
>
> **The unbundled variant is rejected, and not on size.** It is a hundred and forty times
> cheaper and it is still the wrong answer, because a model that has not been downloaded yet
> means a **first image capture with no network fails outright** — not queued under FR-806,
